(1+\vartheta)P_{\Theta}(\varrho\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathcal{J}};\lambda)+\mathcal{L}_{\Theta}\mathbf{D}_{2}(\varrho\boldsymbol{\beta},\varrho\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime})+\delta\|\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}/2\leq(2-\varepsilon)\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{l}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime})+K\lambda^{2}J+(1-\vartheta)P_{\Theta}(\varrho\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}};\lambda)
since
P_{H}\leq P_{\Theta}
.

To get more intuition, let
l(\boldsymbol{\beta})=\|\boldsymbol{X\beta}-\boldsymbol{y}\|_{2}^{2}/2
. Then the above condition simplifies to

(1+\vartheta)P_{\Theta}(\varrho\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathcal{J}};\lambda)+{\mathcal{L}_{\Theta}}\|\varrho\boldsymbol{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}/2\leq(2-\varepsilon^{\prime})\|\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}/2+K\lambda^{2}J+(1-\vartheta)P_{\Theta}(\varrho\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}};\lambda)
with
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\varepsilon+\delta
, or the following sufficient condition (with
K
redefined) for all
\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
:

(1+\vartheta)P_{\Theta}(\varrho\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathcal{J}};\lambda)+\frac{\mathcal{L}_{\Theta}}{2}\|\varrho\boldsymbol{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}\leq K\sqrt{J}\lambda\|\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\gamma}\|_{2}+(1-\vartheta)P_{\Theta}(\varrho\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}};\lambda).

(62)

For lasso, where
P_{\Theta}(\boldsymbol{\beta};\lambda)=\lambda\|\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{1}
, there is a rich collection of regularity conditions in the literature. In this convex case,
\mathcal{L}_{\Theta}=0
and
\varrho
can be arbitrarily large. (
62
) reduces to (with
\vartheta
and
K
redefined and
\lambda
canceled)

\displaystyle(1+\vartheta)\varrho\|\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathcal{J}}\|_{1}\leq K\sqrt{J}\|\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\gamma}\|_{2}+\varrho\|\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}\|_{1},\forall\boldsymbol{\gamma}

(63)

for some
K\geq 0,\vartheta>0
.
Taking
\varrho=c\|\boldsymbol{X}\|_{2}
results in scale invariance with respect to
\boldsymbol{X}
. Let’s compare (
63
) with the restricted eigenvalue (RE) condition and the compatibility condition
[
7
,
59
]
. For given
\mathcal{J}
, the two conditions assume that there exist positive numbers
\kappa
,
\vartheta_{RE}
such that

J\|\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\kappa\|\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathcal{J}}\|_{1}^{2}
(compatibility) or more restrictively,

\|\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\kappa\|\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathcal{J}}\|_{2}^{2}
(RE), for all
\boldsymbol{\gamma}:(1+\vartheta_{RE})\|\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathcal{J}}\|_{1}\geq\|\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}\|_{1}
. Therefore,
(1+\vartheta)\varrho\|\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathcal{J}}\|_{1}\leq K\sqrt{J}\|\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\gamma}\|_{2}\vee\varrho\|\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}\|_{1}
with
K=(1+\vartheta_{RE})/(\varrho\sqrt{\kappa})
,
\vartheta=\vartheta_{RE}
. That is, the RE-type conditions are more demanding than (
63
) (and (
60
)).
Another popular set of regularity conditions is based on restricted strong convexity (RSC). Under a version of RSC condition (and assuming
f
is differentiable),
[
36
, Theorem 1]
showed that
\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\|_{2}^{2}
has a bound of order
\sigma^{2}(J^{*}\log p)/n
for any stationary point
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
. In the lasso case, the condition becomes
\|\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\alpha\|\boldsymbol{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}-\tau\log p\|\boldsymbol{\gamma}\|_{1}^{2}/n
for some constant
\alpha>0
and
\tau\geq 0
, from which it follows that for any
\boldsymbol{\gamma}:(1+\vartheta_{RE})\|\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathcal{J}}\|_{1}\geq\|\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}}\|_{1}
,

\|\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\alpha\|\boldsymbol{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}-\tau(2+\vartheta_{RE})^{2}\frac{\log p}{n}\|\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathcal{J}}\|_{1}^{2}\geq\alpha\|\boldsymbol{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}-\tau(2+\vartheta_{RE})^{2}\frac{J\log p}{n}\|\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathcal{J}}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\kappa^{\prime}\|\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathcal{J}}\|_{2}^{2},
where
\kappa^{\prime}=\alpha-\tau(2+\vartheta_{RE})^{2}(J\log p/n)
. Therefore, when
n\gg J\log p
, RSC implies RE and so is more restrictive than (
63
). See Remark
A.1
in Section
A.12
for an extension to general penalties.

Remark 3

(Technical treatment)
.

A big difference between our work and
[
36
]
is that the latter enforces an
\ell_{1}
-type side constraint, for example,
\|\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{1}\leq R
, in addition to the sparsity-inducing penalty
P
. The use of the constraint is a necessary ingredient of the proofs and the constraint parameter
R
appears in the minimum sample size condition and the error bounds implicitly. However, few practically used algorithms seem to include such an additional
\ell_{1}
constraint.

Our analysis does not need any side constraint, and the resulting error bounds and the oracle inequality hold with no minimum sample size requirement.
In fact, in dealing with a general penalty that may be nonconvex, our treatment of the stochastic term is distinctive from the conventional “
\ell_{1}
fashion” via Hölder’s inequality:
\langle\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}\rangle\leq\|\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\|_{\infty}\|\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{1}
(see, e.g.,
[
10
,
7
,
37
]
). More concretely, applying the union bound to
\|\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\|_{\infty}
will lead to a further upper bound
\|\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{2}^{2}+P(\boldsymbol{\beta};\lambda)
up to multiplicative factors
[
36
]
, while we can bound
\langle\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}\rangle
by the sum of
\|\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{2}^{2}/a
and a light penalty
P_{H}(\boldsymbol{\beta};\lambda)/b
for any
a,b>0
, with a proper choice of
\lambda
.

Remark 4

(Fixed points vs. local minimizers)
.

Targeting at the fixed points of the Bregman surrogate instead of the local minimizers of the original objective seems more reasonable from a statistical perspective. Certainly, if
f
is smooth,
\mathcal{F}
contains more valid solutions (cf. Lemma
4
). But a more important reason is that
\mathcal{F}
can adaptively exclude bad local solutions for some statistical learning problems with severe nonsmoothness and nonconvexity.

For instance, each bridge
\ell_{q}
-penalty (
q:0\leq q<1
)
[
22
]
determines a thresholding
\Theta_{q}
, which is however the solution for infinitely many penalties; picking the particular one constructed from (
48
) that is the lowest and directionally differentiable
[
49
]
, one can repeat the analysis in Theorems
3
,
4
to show provable guarantees for all the fixed points of the iterative
\Theta_{q}
procedure. In contrast, as pointed out by
[
36
]
, the original optimization problem may contain “faulty” local minimizers. In fact, when
q=0
, the
\ell_{0}
-penalized problem
\min_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\|\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{y}\|_{2}^{2}/2+(\lambda^{2}/2)\|\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{0}
(not directionally differentiable)
always
has
\boldsymbol{0}
as a local minimizer which is however a poor estimator as
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}
is large. Switching to the surrogate’s fixed points successfully addresses the issue:
\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\boldsymbol{0}
is a valid fixed point only when
\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{y}
is properly small:
\|\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\infty}\leq\lambda
, or the true signal is inconsequential relative to the maximum noise level.

3.2.2
Statistical analysis of the iterates from Bregman surrogates

We show a nice result for (
52
) in the composite setting: under a regularity condition similar to those in Section
3.2.1
, with high probability, the
t
-th iterate can approach the statistical target within the desired precision geometrically fast, even when
p>n
. Specifically, we add a mild multiple of
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}
to the left-hand side of (
57
) and assume that for some
\delta>0
,
\varepsilon>0
,
\vartheta>0
and large
K\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\beta})+\delta\mathbf{D}_{2}(\boldsymbol{X\beta},\boldsymbol{X\beta}^{*})+\vartheta P_{H}(\varrho(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*});\lambda)+P_{\Theta}(\varrho\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*};\lambda)\\
\leq\,&(2\bar{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{l}-\varrho^{2}\mathcal{L}_{\Theta}\mathbf{D}_{2})(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})+P_{\Theta}(\varrho\boldsymbol{\beta};\lambda)+K\lambda^{2}J(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}),\forall\boldsymbol{\beta}\end{split}

(64)

and
\psi
is differentiable for simplicity.
Recall that (
39
) in Proposition
2
requires
2\bar{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{f}
to dominate
\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}
; (
64
) gives a large-
p
extension of it.

Theorem 5
.

Under the above regularity condition, for
\lambda=A\sigma\sqrt{\log(ep)}/\allowbreak\sqrt{(\delta\wedge\vartheta)\vartheta}
with
A
sufficiently large and
\kappa=1/(1+\varepsilon)
, we have

\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})\leq\kappa^{t}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(0)})+\frac{\kappa}{1-\kappa}(K\lambda^{2}J^{*}-\min_{1\leq s\leq t}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(s)},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(s-1)}))

(65)

for any
t\geq 1
with probability at least
1-Cp^{-cA^{2}}
, where
C,c
are universal positive constants.

The error measure
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})
in (
65
) has
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}
as its first argument and differs from the
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{l}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})
used in (
61
). According to the proof, (
64
) only needs to hold for
\boldsymbol{\beta}=\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(s)}
(
0\leq s\leq t
), and so different starting values may give different values of
\kappa
.
With
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}\geq 0
(which can be realized by stepsize control),
the fast converging statistical error to
\mathcal{O}(\sigma^{2}J^{*}\log(ep))
implies that over-optimization may be unnecessary.
As an example, consider the iterative thresholding procedures with
{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{l}\leq L{\mathbf{D}}_{2}
and
\varrho^{2}>L
. Then (
65
) yields

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\kappa^{t}\frac{\varrho^{2}}{\varrho^{2}-L}\|\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(0)}\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{2\kappa K}{(1-\kappa)(\varrho^{2}-L)}\lambda^{2}J^{*}.

So it is possible to terminate the iterative algorithm before full computational convergence without sacrificing much statistical accuracy. The simulations in Section
C.2
support this point.

Remark 5
.

Theorem
5
reveals the fast decay of the
direct
statistical error between
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)}
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}
.
[
1
]
and
[
36
]
argued a similar point for gradient descent type algorithms, in a somehow indirect manner: (i)
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)}
can approach any globally optimal solution
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
geometrically fast in computation under a combination of an RSC condition and an RSM condition, and (ii) under some regularity conditions, every local minimum point is close enough to the authentic
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}
.
In the RSC condition for (i), the factor proceeding the dominant term
\bar{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{l}
is
1
(there are two different sets of RSC conditions used in Theorem 1 and Theorem 3 of
[
36
]
, the factor
\alpha_{1}
in the second set corresponding to
half
of the
\alpha_{1}
used in the first set). But (
64
) allows it to be
2
.
Moreover, Theorem
5
does not need the extra RSM condition and applies to a broader class of algorithms.
For example, we can show that the statistical error of the LLA algorithm reduces at a linear rate to the desired precision under some regularity conditions; see Proposition
5
and Lemma
A.7
in Section
A.16
.

4
Two acceleration schemes for generalized Bregman surrogates

How to accelerate first-order algorithms without incurring much additional cost per iteration has lately attracted lots of attention in big data applications.
In convex optimization, Nesterov’s momentum techniques prove to be quite effective in that the rate of convergence can be improved from
\mathcal{O}(1/t)
to
\mathcal{O}(1/t^{2})
, which is optimal when using first-order methods on smooth problems
[
41
,
57
,
4
,
32
]
.
This section attempts to extend Nesterov’s
first
and
second
accelerations
[
39
,
40
]
to Bregman-surrogate algorithms. With a possible lack of smoothness or convexity, carefully choosing the relaxation parameters and step sizes is the key, and we will see the benefit of maximizing a quantity
R_{t}/(\theta_{t}^{2}\rho_{t})
at the
t
-th iteration, with
R_{t}
appropriately defined via generalized Bregman notation. We consider the following two broad scenarios to devise the acceleration schemes.

Scenario 1

g(\boldsymbol{\beta};\boldsymbol{\gamma})=f(\boldsymbol{\beta})-\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi_{0}}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})+\rho\mathbf{D}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})
.
This surrogate family includes gradient descent type algorithms. Often, if
\min_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}f(\boldsymbol{\beta})+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})
is easy to solve, so is
\min_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}f(\boldsymbol{\beta})+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})+\rho\mathbf{D}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})
, in which case
\psi_{0}=-\psi
.

Scenario 2

g(\boldsymbol{\beta};\boldsymbol{\gamma})=f(\boldsymbol{\beta})-\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi_{0}}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})+\rho\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\phi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})
.
This gives a more general class than the first one.

This section assumes that
f
,
\psi_{0}
,
\phi
,
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi_{0}}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\gamma})
,
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi_{0}}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\gamma})
are directionally differentiable given any
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
. We introduce a convenient notation
\mathbf{C}_{\psi}
defined for any
\psi
as follows

\mathbf{C}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta},\theta)=\theta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha})+(1-\theta)\psi(\boldsymbol{\beta})-\psi(\theta\boldsymbol{\alpha}+(1-\theta)\boldsymbol{\beta}),

(66)

where
0\leq\theta\leq 1
.
Like
{\mathbf{\Delta}}
,
\mathbf{C}
is a linear operator of
\psi
and its nonnegativity means convexity. Some connections between
{\mathbf{\Delta}}
and
\mathbf{C}
are given below.

Lemma 5
.

Let
\psi
be directionally differentiable. (i)
\mathbf{C}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta},\theta)=(1-\theta)\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\theta\boldsymbol{\alpha}+(1-\theta)\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\alpha})
for any
\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}
and
\theta\in[0,1]
.
(ii)
\mathbf{C}_{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\alpha})}=\mathbf{C}_{\psi}
if
\psi
is differentiable at
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
.

An acceleration scheme of the second kind

Scenario 2 is of our primary interest since it applies more broadly. Below, we modify the surrogate and define an iterative algorithm (not a descent method)
that involves three sequences
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(t)}
,
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)}
,
\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)}
starting at
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(0)}=\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(0)}
:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)}=(1-\theta_{t})\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)}+\theta_{t}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(t)}

(67a)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(t+1)}{=}\mathop{{\arg}{\min}}f(\boldsymbol{\beta}){-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi_{0}}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)}){+}\mu_{0}{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{\phi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)}){+}\theta_{t}\rho_{t}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\phi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(t)})

(67b)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)}=(1-\theta_{t})\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)}+\theta_{t}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(t+1)},

(67c)

for some
\mu_{0}\geq 0
,
\theta_{t}\in(0,1]
,
\rho_{t}>0
(
\forall t\geq 0
), to be chosen later. Notice the extra GBF term
\mu_{0}{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{\phi}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)})
in (
67b
) in addition to
{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{\phi}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(t)})
.
The design of relaxation parameters
\theta_{t}
and inverse step size parameters
\rho_{t},\mu_{0}
holds the key to acceleration.
Let

\displaystyle\bar{\psi}_{0}=\psi_{0}-\mu_{0}\phi.

(68)

We advocate the following line search criterion

\displaystyle\begin{split}&R_{t}:=\theta_{t}^{2}\rho_{t}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\phi}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(t)})-\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\bar{\psi}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)})+(1-\theta_{t})\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\bar{\psi}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)})\\
&\qquad\ +\mathbf{C}_{f(\cdot)-\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\bar{\psi}_{0}}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)})}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)},\theta_{t})\geq 0,\end{split}

(69a)

\displaystyle\frac{\theta_{t}^{2}}{1-\theta_{t}}=\frac{\theta_{t-1}(\rho_{t-1}\theta_{t-1}+\mu_{0})}{\rho_{t}},\ t\geq 1.

(69b)

The update of the relaxation parameter involves
\rho
and
\mu
as well.

Theorem
6
presents two error bounds without assuming convexity or smoothness, and shows in general the reasonability of (
69a
).

Theorem 6
.

Let
\rho_{t}
be any positive sequence. Consider the algorithm defined by (
67a
)–(
67c
) and (
69b
).
Let
\mathcal{E}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\beta})=\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\bar{\psi}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)})+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{f(\cdot)-\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi_{0}}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)})}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(t+1)})+(\mu_{0}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\phi}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)})-\phi(\cdot)}\allowbreak+\theta_{t}\rho_{t}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\phi}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(t)})-\phi(\cdot)})(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(t+1)})
.

(i) When
\mu_{0}=0
, for any
\boldsymbol{\beta}
and
T\geq 0
,

\begin{split}&\frac{f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(T+1)})-f(\boldsymbol{\beta})}{\theta_{T}^{2}\rho_{T}}+T{\cdot}\mathop{\mathrm{avg}}_{0\leq t\leq T}\frac{\mathcal{E}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\beta})}{\theta_{t}\rho_{t}}+T{\cdot}\mathop{\mathrm{avg}}_{0\leq t\leq T}\frac{R_{t}}{\theta_{t}^{2}\rho_{t}}\\
\leq\,&\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\phi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(0)})-\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\phi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(T+1)})+\frac{1-\theta_{0}}{\theta_{0}^{2}\rho_{0}}\big[f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(0)})-f(\boldsymbol{\beta})\big].\end{split}

(70)

(ii) Moreover, given any
\mu_{0}\geq 0
,

\begin{split}&f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(T+1)})-f(\boldsymbol{\beta})+\theta_{T}^{2}(\rho_{T}+\frac{\mu_{0}}{\theta_{T}})\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\phi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(T+1)})\\
&+{\mbox{$\Sigma$}}_{t=0}^{T}\big(\Pi_{s=t+1}^{T}(1-\theta_{s})\big)(R_{t}+\theta_{t}\mathcal{E}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\beta}))\\
\leq\,&\Big({\mbox{$\prod$}}_{t=1}^{T}(1-\theta_{t})\Big)\big[(1-\theta_{0})(f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(0)})-f(\boldsymbol{\beta}))+\theta_{0}^{2}\rho_{0}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\phi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(0)})\big]\end{split}

(71)

for all
\boldsymbol{\beta}
and
T\geq 0
,
where by convention,
\prod_{s=l}^{u}a_{s}=1
as
l>u
.

First, we make a discussion of the results for convex optimization. Assume
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\phi}\geq\sigma\mathbf{D}_{2}
for some
\sigma>0
. With the additional knowledge that
f(\cdot)-\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\bar{\psi}_{0}}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)})
is convex and
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\bar{\psi}_{0}}\leq L_{\bar{\psi}_{0}}\mathbf{D}_{2}
for some
L_{\bar{\psi}_{0}}\geq 0
, (
69a
) is implied by

\theta_{t}^{2}(\rho_{t}-L_{\bar{\psi}_{0}}/\sigma)\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\phi}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(t)})+(1-\theta_{t})\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\bar{\psi}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)})\geq 0.

(72)

So when
f
is convex, criterion (
69
) is satisfied by
\rho_{t}=\rho\geq L_{\bar{\psi}_{0}}/\sigma,\psi_{0}=f,\mu_{0}=0
and
\theta_{t+1}=(\sqrt{\theta_{t}^{4}+4\theta_{t}^{2}}-\theta_{t}^{2})/2
, degenerating to Nesterov’s second method
[
40
,
57
]
, and the convergence rate is of order
\mathcal{O}(1/T^{2})
according to (
70
) and (
75
). The second conclusion tells more when strong convexity (or restricted strong convexity) arises. Given a convex
f
satisfying
\mu{\mathbf{D}}_{\phi}\leq{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{f}\leq L{\mathbf{D}}_{\phi}
with
0<\mu\leq L
and
\phi
differentiable, taking
\psi_{0}=f
,
\mu_{0}=\mu
, and
\rho_{t}=L-\mu
ensures
\mathcal{E}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\beta})=\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{f-\mu\phi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)})+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{f(\cdot)-\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{f}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)})}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(t+1)})\geq\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{f-\mu\phi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)})\geq 0
and
R_{t}\geq\theta_{t}^{2}\rho_{t}{\mathbf{D}}_{\phi}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(t)})-\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\bar{\psi}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)})\geq\theta_{t}^{2}(\rho_{t}+\mu_{0}-L)\sigma{\mathbf{D}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(t)})=0
. According to (
69b
),
the following choice

\theta_{t}=\theta_{0}=\frac{2}{\sqrt{4\kappa-3}+1}\mbox{ with }\kappa=L/\mu

(73)

suffices, and the optimization problem to solve in (
67b
) becomes

\displaystyle\min f(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)})+\delta f(\boldsymbol{\beta};\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)})+\mu{\mathbf{D}}_{\phi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)}){+}\frac{2(L-\mu)}{\sqrt{4\kappa-3}+1}{\mathbf{D}}_{\phi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(t)}).

(74)

From (
71
), both
f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(T+1)})-f(\boldsymbol{\beta})
and
{\mathbf{D}}_{\phi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(T+1)})
enjoy a linear convergence with rate parameter
\frac{\sqrt{4\kappa-3}-1}{\sqrt{4\kappa-3}+1}
, or an iteration complexity of
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\kappa}\log(1/\epsilon))
,
significantly faster than
\mathcal{O}(\kappa\log(1/\epsilon))
in Proposition
2
. Hence (
67
), (
69
) can achieve rate-optimality in various convex scenarios. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first “all-in-one” form of the
second
acceleration that adapts.

The proposed algorithm can even go beyond convexity.
As a demonstration, let us apply the acceleration to the iterative quantile-thresholding procedure (cf. Example
2
) for solving the feature screening problem:
\min l(\boldsymbol{\beta})=\|\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{2}^{2}/2\mbox{ s.t. }\|\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{0}\leq q
, which is nonconvex. Here,
q
is bounded above by
p
but may be larger than
n
. Take
\phi=\|\cdot\|_{2}^{2}/2
,
\mu_{0}=0
and
\psi_{0}(\boldsymbol{\beta})=l(\boldsymbol{\beta})-\mathcal{L}\phi(\boldsymbol{\beta})
for some
\mathcal{L}\geq 0
.
Given any
s\leq p
and
\boldsymbol{X}
, define the restricted isometry number
\rho_{+}(s)

[
12
]
that satisfies

\|\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\rho_{+}(s)\|\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{2}^{2}
,
\forall\boldsymbol{\beta}:\|\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{0}\leq s
, which can be much smaller than
\|\boldsymbol{X}\|_{2}^{2}
as
s
is small.

Corollary 2
.

Assume
q
is set larger than the target
\|\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\|_{0}
with the ratio denoted by
r
. Then for any
\mathcal{L}\geq\rho_{+}(2q)/\sqrt{r}
, there exists a
universal

\rho_{t}
(
\rho_{t}=\rho_{+}(2q)(1-1/\sqrt{r})
, say), thereby
\theta_{t+1}=(\sqrt{\theta_{t}^{4}+4\theta_{t}^{2}}-\theta_{t}^{2})/2
, such that the accelerated iterative quantile-thresholding according to (
67a
)–(
67c
) satisfies
l(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(T+1)})-l(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})+\min_{0\leq t\leq T}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi_{0}}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)})\leq A/T^{2}

for all
T\geq 0
, where
A
is independent of
T
.

The proof of the corollary shows the power of an
accumulative

R_{t}
-control, and applies more generally: if the objective function
f(\boldsymbol{\beta})
, possibly nonconvex, can be written as the sum of a convex function
l(\boldsymbol{\beta})
with
{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{l}\leq L{\mathbf{D}}_{2}
and a function
P(\boldsymbol{\beta})
that can be lifted:
{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{P}+\mathcal{L}_{0}{\mathbf{D}}_{2}\geq 0
for some finite
\mathcal{L}_{0}\geq 0
, then one can utilize a
\psi_{0}
as
l-0.6\mathcal{L}_{0}\|\cdot\|_{2}^{2}
and a universal
\rho_{t}
to fulfill
T{\cdot}\mathop{\mathrm{avg}}_{t\leq T}{R_{t}}/({\theta_{t}^{2}\rho_{t}})\geq 0
in (
70
) (although not every
R_{t}
is necessarily nonnegative) so as to attain an
\mathcal{O}(1/T^{2})
error bound. See Remark
A.3
in Section
A.14
.

Of course, a time-varying
\rho_{t}
can provide finer control, and the theorem does not limit
\rho_{t}
to be constant.
In fact, under
\mu_{0}=0
, as long as
\rho_{t}/\rho_{t-1}\geq 1-(at+ab+1)/(t+b-1)^{2}
(
t\geq 1
) for some constants
a,b
:
a>-2,b\geq a+1
, induction based on (
69b
) gives
\theta_{t}\leq(a+2)/(t+b)
and
\sum_{t=0}^{T}\rho_{T}/(\rho_{t}\theta_{t})\geq(T+c_{1})^{2}/(a+2)^{2}+c_{2}
(with constants
c_{i}
dependent on
a,b
) for any
t\geq 1
, from which it follows that

\displaystyle\theta_{T}^{2}=\mathcal{O}(1/T^{2})\mbox{~~and~~}T\cdot\mathrm{avg}_{0\leq t\leq T}(1/(\rho_{t}\theta_{t}))\geq\mathcal{O}(T^{2}/\rho_{T}).

(75)

Now, under
R_{t}\geq 0
or just
\sum_{t=0}^{T}R_{t}/(\theta_{t}^{2}\rho_{t})\geq 0
, (
70
) gives

f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(T+1)})-f(\boldsymbol{\beta})+\min_{0\leq t\leq T}\mathcal{E}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\beta})\allowbreak\leq\mathcal{O}(\rho_{T}/T^{2})
for any
\boldsymbol{\beta}
.
Typically, (
69a
) involves a line search. If the condition fails for the current value of
\rho_{t}
, one can set
\rho_{t}=\alpha\rho_{t}
for some
\alpha>1
and recalculate
\theta_{t}
,
\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)}
,
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(t+1)}
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)}
according to (
69b
) and (
67
) to verify it again. In implementation, it is wise to limit the number of searches at each iteration (denoted by
M
) to control the per-iteration complexity. If (
69a
) does not hold after
m
times of search, we simply pick the
\rho_{t}
that gives the largest
R_{t}/(\theta_{t}^{2}\rho_{t})
based on Theorem
6
. Some details are in Algorithm
B.1
. In simulation studies, letting
M=3
,
\alpha=2
already shows excellent performance; see Figure
C.5
and Figure
C.6
.

An acceleration scheme of the first kind

For the algorithms falling into Scenario 1, we can alternatively consider two sequences of iterates generated by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)}+\{\rho_{t-1}\theta_{t}(1-\theta_{t-1})/(\rho_{t-1}\theta_{t-1}+\mu_{0})\}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t-1)}),

(76a)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\arg\min}f(\boldsymbol{\beta}){-}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi_{0}}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)}){+}\mu_{0}{\mathbf{D}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)}){+}\rho_{t}\mathbf{D}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)}),

(76b)

for some
\mu_{0}\geq 0
,
\theta_{t}\in(0,1]
,
\rho_{t}>0
for all
t\geq 0
, and we force
\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(0)}=\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(0)}
. (
76a
), (
76b
) give a new first type acceleration, and notably, the novel update of
\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)}
involves
\rho_{t-1}
. When
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)}=\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)}
one stops the algorithm and obtains a fixed point with provable statistical guarantees as shown in Section
3.2.1
.

Similar to (
68
), let
\bar{\psi}_{0}=\psi_{0}-\mu_{0}\|\cdot\|_{2}^{2}/2
. Define
the line search criterion

\displaystyle R_{t}:=(\rho_{t}\mathbf{D}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\bar{\psi}_{0}})(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)})+(1-\theta_{t})\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\bar{\psi}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)})\geq 0,

(77a)

\displaystyle\frac{\theta_{t}^{2}}{1-\theta_{t}}=\frac{\theta_{t-1}(\rho_{t-1}\theta_{t-1}+\mu_{0})}{\rho_{t}},~\theta_{t}\geq 0,~\rho_{t}>0,~t\geq 1.

(77b)

Note that
R_{t}
is defined differently from (
69a
). The following theorem reveals the importance of maximizing
R_{t}
in each iteration step when performing possibly nonconvex optimization.

Theorem 7
.

Given any
\rho_{t}>0

(t\geq 0)
, consider the algorithm defined by (
76a
), (
76b
), and (
77b
). Let
\mathcal{E}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\beta})=\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\bar{\psi}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)})+\{\mathbf{C}_{f(\cdot)-\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi_{0}}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)})}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)},\theta_{t})+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{f(\cdot)-\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi_{0}}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)})}\allowbreak(\theta_{t}\boldsymbol{\beta}+(1-\theta_{t})\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)})\}/\theta_{t}
.

(i) When
\mu_{0}=0
, we have

\begin{split}&\frac{f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(T+1)})-f(\boldsymbol{\beta})}{\theta_{T}^{2}\rho_{T}}+T{\cdot}\mathop{\mathrm{avg}}_{0\leq t\leq T}\frac{\mathcal{E}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\beta})}{\theta_{t}\rho_{t}}+T{\cdot}\mathop{\mathrm{avg}}_{0\leq t\leq T}\frac{R_{t}}{\theta_{t}^{2}\rho_{t}}\\
\leq\,&\mathbf{D}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(0)})+\frac{1-\theta_{0}}{\theta_{0}^{2}\rho_{0}}\big[f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(0)})-f(\boldsymbol{\beta})\big]\ \mbox{ for any }\boldsymbol{\beta}\mbox{ and }T\geq 0.\end{split}

(ii) Moreover, given any
\mu_{0}\geq 0
, for all
\boldsymbol{\beta}
and
T\geq 0
,

\begin{split}&f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(T+1)})-f(\boldsymbol{\beta})+\theta_{T}^{2}(\rho_{T}+\frac{\mu_{0}}{\theta_{T}}){\mathbf{D}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\beta},({\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(T+1)}}{-}(1{-}\theta_{T+1})\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(T+1)})/{\theta_{T+1}})\\
&\quad+{\mbox{$\Sigma$}}_{t=0}^{T}\big(\Pi_{s=t+1}^{T}(1-\theta_{s})\big)(R_{t}+\theta_{t}\mathcal{E}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\beta}))\\
\leq\,&\Big({\mbox{$\prod$}}_{t=1}^{T}(1-\theta_{t})\Big)\big[(1-\theta_{0})(f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(0)})-f(\boldsymbol{\beta}))+\theta_{0}^{2}\rho_{0}{\mathbf{D}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(0)})\big].\end{split}

Again, the new proposal of the iterate and parameter updates adapts to various situations, with
\mu_{0}
(which can be a sequence
\mu_{t}
, cf. Remark
A.2
) measuring the degree of convexity (or restricted convexity in a nonconvex composite problem).
For example, when
f
is convex and
L
-strongly smooth,
\mu_{0}=0
,
\rho_{t}=L
,
\psi_{0}=f
, and
\theta_{t+1}=(\sqrt{\theta_{t}^{4}+4\theta_{t}^{2}}-\theta_{t}^{2})/2
make (
77
) hold, corresponding to Nesterov’s first method. Interestingly, if
f
is
\mu
-strongly convex, the associated standard momentum update
\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)}=\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)}+\theta_{t}(\theta_{t-1}^{-1}-1)(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t-1)})
only attains a linear rate at
1-1/\kappa
(
\kappa=L/\mu
) (cf. Remark
A.4
), showing
no
theoretical advantage over the plain gradient descent. (
76
) fixes the issue: with
\mu_{0}=\mu
,
\rho_{t}=L-\mu
,
\theta_{t}={2}/({\sqrt{4\kappa-3}+1})
, an accelerated linear rate parameter is obtained as
({\sqrt{4\kappa-3}-1})/\allowbreak({\sqrt{4\kappa-3}+1})(\leq 1-\sqrt{3/(4\kappa)})
. (When
\mu_{0}
is unknown, (
76b
) based on the
split
L=\rho_{t}+\mu_{t}
is still advantageous over the classical acceleration with
\rho_{t}=L
.) We proved these error bounds by use of GBFs, which is perhaps more straightforward than Nesterov’s ingenious proof based on
the notion of estimate sequence, and more importantly, (
76
), (
77
) provide a universal “all-in-one” form, instead of separate schemes in different situations
[
41
]
.

Theorem
7
accommodates diverse choices of the parameters
\psi_{0},\mu_{0},\rho_{t}
,
\theta_{t}
and is motivating in the nonconvex composite setup. Consider, for example,
\min f(\boldsymbol{\beta})=\|\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{2}^{2}/2+P_{\Theta}(\varrho\boldsymbol{\beta};\lambda)
. Because the objective is nonconvex when
p>n
and
\mathcal{L}_{\Theta}>0
, how to accelerate the associated iterative thresholding procedure is an unconventional problem. From the studies in Section
3.2
, we have learned that a sparsity-inducing penalty with a properly large threshold to suppress the noise can result in strong convexity in a restricted sense. We can then use a surrogate
f(\boldsymbol{\beta})+(\rho{\mathbf{D}}_{2}-{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{\psi_{0}})(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-})
where
\psi_{0}(\boldsymbol{\beta})=\|\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{2}^{2}/2-\varrho^{2}\mathcal{L}_{\Theta}\|\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{2}^{2}/2
and
\mu_{0}=0
. Since
f(\cdot)-\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi_{0}}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\gamma})
is convex (cf. Lemma
A.3
),
\mathcal{E}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\beta})\geq\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi_{0}}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)})
.
Moreover, thanks to the sparsity in
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)}
, and thus
\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)}
,
\boldsymbol{X}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)})
involves just a small number of features. So with an incoherent design, a properly small
\varrho
can make
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi_{0}}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)})\geq 0
. Now, taking a constant
\rho_{t}
as large as, for instance,
\|\boldsymbol{X}\|_{2}^{2}-\varrho^{2}\mathcal{L}_{\Theta}
, may yield a convergence rate of order
\mathcal{O}(1/t^{2})
. (Actually, linear convergence may result from the restricted strong convexity under some regularity conditions.)
More generally, different
\rho_{t}
’s are allowed in the theorem: (
75
) is still secured with just, say,
\rho_{t}/\rho_{t-1}\geq 1-(t+3)/(t+1)^{2}
. A line search can be used to determine a proper sequence
\rho_{t}
; see Algorithm
B.2
for more details.

The proposed accelerations of the first kind and of the second kind can be utilized in a wide range of problems. Because they are momentum based, the original algorithms need not be substantially modified to have an improved
iteration complexity, and the two theorems proved in this section apply in any dimensions with no design coherence restrictions. Another delightful fact is that our “all-in-one” forms update the iterates adaptively according to the degree of convexity
\mu_{0}\geq 0,
which can be relaxed to a sequence of local measures

\mu_{t}
(Remark
A.2
). With a line search to get properly large
\mu_{t}
, this could be helpful in high dimensional sparse learning problems which may or may
not
have
restricted strong convexity (the associated parameter often hard to determine in theory).

5
Summary

This paper studied the class of iterative algorithms derived from GBF-defined surrogates with a possible lack of convexity and/or smoothness. These surrogates differ from the MM surrogates frequently used in statistical computation, in that they gain additional first-order degeneracy and may drop the majorization requirement. GBFs have interesting connections to the densities in the exponential family and possess some idempotence properties that are useful for studying iterative algorithms.

The GBF calculus built by the lemmas not only facilitates optimization error analysis but can be bound to the empirical process theory for nonasymptotic statistical analysis (cf. Sections
3.2
and
A.18
).
In addition to obtaining some insightful results in the realm of convex optimization,
we were able to build universal global convergence rates for a broad class of Bregman-surrogate algorithms for nonsmooth nonconvex optimization. Moreover, in the nonconvex composite setting that is of great interest in high dimensional statistics, we found that the sequence of iterates generated by Bregman surrogates can approach the statistical truth at a linear rate even when
p>n
, and the obtained fixed points enjoy oracle inequalities with essentially the optimal order of statistical accuracy, under some regularity conditions less demanding than those used in the literature. Finally, we devised two “all-in-one” acceleration schemes with novel updates of the iterates and relaxation and stepsize parameters, and some sharp theoretical bounds were shown without assuming smoothness or convexity.

A
Proofs

We list some notation and symbols that are used in the proofs.
Given a directionally differentiable function
\psi
,
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})=\psi(\boldsymbol{\beta})-\psi(\boldsymbol{\gamma})-\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\gamma};\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})
,
\bar{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})=(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\gamma},\boldsymbol{\beta}))/2
, and
{\mathrel{\mathop{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\smallsetminus$}\vss}}}_{\psi}}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})=\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\gamma},\boldsymbol{\beta})
. We occasionally denote
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})
by
\boldsymbol{\Delta}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})
when there is no ambiguity.
The classes of continuous functions and continuously differentiable functions are denoted by
\mathcal{C}^{0}
and
\mathcal{C}^{1}
, respectively. Recall that all functions are assumed to be defined on a vector space unless otherwise mentioned.

Definition A.1
.

We call
\xi
a sub-Gaussian random variable if and only if there exist constants
C,c>0
such that
\mathbb{P}\{|\xi|\geq t\}\leq Ce^{-ct^{2}},\forall t>0
. The scale (or
\psi_{2}
-norm) of
\xi
is defined by
\sigma(\xi)=\inf\{\sigma>0:\mathbb{E}\exp(\xi^{2}/\sigma^{2})\leq 2\}
. More generally,
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
is called a sub-Gaussian random vector with scale bounded by
\sigma
if all one-dimensional marginals
\langle\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\alpha}\rangle
are sub-Gaussian satisfying
\|\langle\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\alpha}\rangle\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq\sigma\|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\|_{2}
,
\forall\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
.

Definition A.2
.

We call
d
a pseudo-metric if it satisfies
d(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\eta}_{2})=d(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{2},\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1})\geq 0
and
d(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\eta}_{2})\leq d(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\eta}_{3})+d(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{2},\boldsymbol{\eta}_{3})
, for all
\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\eta}_{2},\boldsymbol{\eta}_{3}
.

We state a first-order optimality condition satisfied by all local minimizers of
f
that is directionally differentiable. The result is basic and we omit the proof.
It holds the key to deriving the so-called “basic inequality” in a variety of statistical learning problems.

Lemma A.1
.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{p}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a real-valued function and
C\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}
be a convex set. Suppose that
f
is directionally differentiable at
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{o}
that is a local minimizer to the problem
\min_{\boldsymbol{\beta}\in C}f(\boldsymbol{\beta})
. Then
\delta f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{o};\boldsymbol{h})\geq 0
with
\boldsymbol{h}=\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{o}
or
f(\boldsymbol{\beta})-f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{o})\geq{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{f}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{o})
for all
\boldsymbol{\beta}\in C
.

A.1
Proof of Lemma
1

(i) This property is straightforward by definition:

\begin{split}&\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{a\psi+b\varphi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})\\
=\,&(a\psi+b\varphi)(\boldsymbol{\beta})-(a\psi+b\varphi)(\boldsymbol{\gamma})-\delta(a\psi+b\varphi)(\boldsymbol{\gamma};\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})\\
=\,&a\big[\psi(\boldsymbol{\beta})-\psi(\boldsymbol{\gamma})-\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\gamma};\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})\big]+b\big[\varphi(\boldsymbol{\beta})-\varphi(\boldsymbol{\gamma})-\delta\varphi(\boldsymbol{\gamma};\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})\big]\\
=\,&a\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})+b\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\varphi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}).\end{split}

(ii) From
[
45
, Theorem 23.1]
, the convexity of
\psi
implies the directional differentiability of
\psi
and the positively homogenous convexity of
\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\beta};\cdot)
for any given
\boldsymbol{\beta}
, and we can write

\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\beta};\boldsymbol{h})=\inf_{\epsilon>0}\frac{\psi(\boldsymbol{\beta}+\epsilon\boldsymbol{h})-\psi(\boldsymbol{\beta})}{\epsilon}.

(A.1)

Putting
\epsilon=1
and
\boldsymbol{h}=\boldsymbol{\gamma}-\boldsymbol{\beta}
in (
A.1
) gives
\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\beta};\boldsymbol{\gamma}-\boldsymbol{\beta})\leq\psi(\boldsymbol{\gamma})-\psi(\boldsymbol{\beta})
, thus
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\gamma},\boldsymbol{\beta})\geq 0
.

Conversely, suppose that
\psi
defined on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is directionally differentiable (
\delta\psi
exists and is finite), thus radially continuous, and
{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{\psi}\geq 0
. For any
\boldsymbol{s}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}:\langle\boldsymbol{s}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\boldsymbol{h}\rangle\leq\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\beta};\boldsymbol{h}),\boldsymbol{s}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}:\langle\boldsymbol{s}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}},\boldsymbol{h}\rangle\leq\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\gamma};\boldsymbol{h})\ \forall\boldsymbol{h}
,

\displaystyle\psi(\boldsymbol{\beta})-\psi(\boldsymbol{\gamma})-\langle\boldsymbol{s}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}},\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}\rangle\geq{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})\geq 0,

(A.2)

\displaystyle\psi(\boldsymbol{\gamma})-\psi(\boldsymbol{\beta})-\langle\boldsymbol{s}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\boldsymbol{\gamma}-\boldsymbol{\beta}\rangle\geq{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\gamma},\boldsymbol{\beta})\geq 0.

(A.3)

Adding them together gives
\langle\boldsymbol{s}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{s}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}},\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}\rangle\geq 0
. This indicates the monotone property of the Clarke-Rockafellar subdifferential of
\psi
, thereby its convexity according to
[
16
]
.

(iii) To show the first result, notice that
{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{\psi\circ\varphi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})-{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{\psi}(\varphi(\boldsymbol{\beta}),\varphi(\boldsymbol{\gamma}))=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}\{\psi(\varphi(\boldsymbol{\gamma})\allowbreak+\epsilon(\varphi(\boldsymbol{\beta})-\varphi(\boldsymbol{\gamma})))-\psi(\varphi(\boldsymbol{\gamma}+\epsilon(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})))\}/\epsilon=\langle\nabla\psi(\varphi(\boldsymbol{\gamma})),\varphi(\boldsymbol{\beta})-\varphi(\boldsymbol{\gamma})\rangle-\delta(\psi\circ\varphi)(\boldsymbol{\gamma};\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})
. From
\psi\in\mathcal{C}^{1}
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}^{0}
,

\displaystyle\delta(\psi\circ\varphi)(\boldsymbol{\gamma};\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})=

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}\{\psi(\varphi(\boldsymbol{\gamma})+(\varphi(\boldsymbol{\gamma}+\epsilon(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}))-\varphi(\boldsymbol{\gamma})))-\psi(\varphi(\boldsymbol{\gamma}))\}/{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}\langle\nabla\psi(\varphi(\boldsymbol{\gamma})),\varphi(\boldsymbol{\gamma}+\epsilon(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}))-\varphi(\boldsymbol{\gamma})/\epsilon\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\nabla\psi(\varphi(\boldsymbol{\gamma})),\delta\varphi(\boldsymbol{\gamma};\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})\rangle.

Using the definition of
{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{\varphi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})
(the componentwise extension), we obtain the conclusion.

Next, we prove the second result. Let
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{p}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the linear function
\varphi(\boldsymbol{\beta})=\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}+\boldsymbol{\alpha}
with its Jacobian matrix
\,\mathrm{D}\varphi(\boldsymbol{\beta}):=[\,\mathrm{D}_{j}\varphi_{i}(x)]=\boldsymbol{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}
. By definition,
{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{\psi\circ\varphi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})=\psi(\varphi(\boldsymbol{\beta}))-\psi(\varphi(\boldsymbol{\gamma}))-\delta(\psi\circ\varphi)(\boldsymbol{\gamma};\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})
and

\displaystyle\delta(\psi\circ\varphi)(\boldsymbol{\gamma};\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})=

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}\{\psi(\varphi(\boldsymbol{\gamma}+\epsilon(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})))-\psi(\varphi(\boldsymbol{\gamma}))\}/\epsilon

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}\{\psi(\varphi(\boldsymbol{\gamma})+\epsilon\,\mathrm{D}\varphi(\boldsymbol{\gamma})(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})))-\psi(\varphi(\boldsymbol{\gamma}))\}/\epsilon

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\delta\psi(\varphi(\boldsymbol{\gamma});\,\mathrm{D}\varphi(\boldsymbol{\gamma})(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}))=\delta\psi(\varphi(\boldsymbol{\gamma});\varphi(\boldsymbol{\beta})-\varphi(\boldsymbol{\gamma})),

from which it follows that

{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{\psi\circ\varphi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})={\mathbf{\Delta}}_{\psi}(\varphi(\boldsymbol{\beta}),\varphi(\boldsymbol{\gamma}))
.

(iv) From Theorem 11 in
[
25
]
, for any continuous function
f
with finite Dini derivative
D^{+}f(x):=\mathop{\lim\sup}_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}(f(x+\epsilon)-f(x))/\epsilon
, if
D^{+}f(x)
is integrable over
[a,b]
,
f(b)-f(a)=\int_{a}^{b}D^{+}f(x)\text{d}x
.
By definition,
\psi
is continuous when restricted to the line segment
[\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}]
(radial continuity). It follows that

\begin{split}\psi(\boldsymbol{\beta})-\psi(\boldsymbol{\gamma})&=\psi\big(\boldsymbol{\gamma}+t(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})\big)\Big|_{t=0}^{1}\\
&=\int_{0}^{1}\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big[\psi\big(\boldsymbol{\gamma}+(t+\epsilon)(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})\big)-\psi\big(\boldsymbol{\gamma}+t(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})\big)\Big]\text{d}t\\
&=\int_{0}^{1}\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big[\psi\big(\boldsymbol{\gamma}+t(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})+\epsilon(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})\big)-\psi\big(\boldsymbol{\gamma}+t(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})\big)\Big]\text{d}t\\
&=\int_{0}^{1}\delta\psi\big(\boldsymbol{\gamma}+t(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma});\,\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}\big)\text{d}t.\end{split}

Hence,
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}
can be formulated by

\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})=\int_{0}^{1}\Big[\delta\psi\big(\boldsymbol{\gamma}+t(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma});\,\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}\big)-\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\gamma};\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})\Big]\,\mathrm{d}t.

A.2
Proof of Lemma
2

(i)
First, if
\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};\cdot-\boldsymbol{\alpha})
is directionally differentiable, then

\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})-\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\alpha})}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})=\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};\cdot-\boldsymbol{\alpha})}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})

(A.4)

for any
\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}
.
In fact,
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})-\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\alpha})}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})=\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi(\cdot)-\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\alpha})}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})=\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha})+\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};\cdot-\boldsymbol{\alpha})}\allowbreak(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})=\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};\cdot-\boldsymbol{\alpha})}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})
.

Accordingly, when
\psi
is convex, which means
\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};\cdot-\boldsymbol{\alpha})
is convex as well (cf. Section
A.1
),
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};\cdot-\boldsymbol{\alpha})}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})\geq 0
by Lemma
1
. The result under concavity can be similarly proved.

(ii)
Let

\displaystyle q(\cdot;\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};\cdot-\boldsymbol{\alpha}).

(A.5)

We want to show for
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\theta\boldsymbol{\beta}+(1-\theta)\boldsymbol{\gamma}
with
\theta\leq 0
or
\theta\geq 1
,
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{q(\cdot;\boldsymbol{\alpha})}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})

is well-defined and equals 0. This is intuitive due to the linearity of
q
when restricted to
[\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}]
, assuming
\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\alpha}
and
\boldsymbol{\gamma}-\boldsymbol{\alpha}
are positively collinear.

To verify it, by definition,

\begin{split}\delta q(\cdot;\boldsymbol{\alpha})(\boldsymbol{\gamma};\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})=&\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}[q(\boldsymbol{\gamma}+\epsilon(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma});\boldsymbol{\alpha})-q(\boldsymbol{\gamma};\boldsymbol{\alpha})]/\epsilon\\
=&\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}[\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};\boldsymbol{\gamma}+\epsilon(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})-\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};\boldsymbol{\gamma}-\boldsymbol{\alpha})]/\epsilon\\
=&\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}[\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};(\theta-\epsilon)(\boldsymbol{\gamma}-\boldsymbol{\beta}))-\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};\theta(\boldsymbol{\gamma}-\boldsymbol{\beta}))]/\epsilon\\
=&\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}[\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};(\epsilon-\theta)(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}))-\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};(-\theta)(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}))]/\epsilon,\end{split}

and so with
\theta>0
,

\begin{split}\delta q(\cdot;\boldsymbol{\alpha})(\boldsymbol{\gamma};\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})=&\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}[(\theta-\epsilon)\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};\boldsymbol{\gamma}-\boldsymbol{\beta})-\theta\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};\boldsymbol{\gamma}-\boldsymbol{\beta})]/\epsilon\\
=&-\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};\boldsymbol{\gamma}-\boldsymbol{\beta}),\end{split}

and with
\theta\leq 0
,

\begin{split}\delta q(\cdot;\boldsymbol{\alpha})(\boldsymbol{\gamma};\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})=&\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}[(\epsilon-\theta)\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}))-(-\theta)\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}))]/\epsilon\\
=&\,\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}).\end{split}

The above derivation also guarantees the existence of
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\alpha})}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})
.
Now, as
\theta\geq 1
,
\langle\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\gamma}-\boldsymbol{\beta}\rangle\geq 0
and so
q(\boldsymbol{\beta};\boldsymbol{\alpha})-q(\boldsymbol{\gamma};\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\delta q(\cdot;\boldsymbol{\alpha})(\boldsymbol{\gamma};\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})=\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};\boldsymbol{\gamma}-\boldsymbol{\alpha})+\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};\boldsymbol{\gamma}-\boldsymbol{\beta})=0
. As
\theta\leq 0
,
\langle\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\gamma}-\boldsymbol{\beta}\rangle\leq 0
and
q(\boldsymbol{\beta};\boldsymbol{\alpha})-q(\boldsymbol{\gamma};\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\delta q(\cdot;\boldsymbol{\alpha})(\boldsymbol{\gamma};\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})=\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};\boldsymbol{\gamma}-\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})=0
.

(iii) By definition, we have

\displaystyle\ \delta q(\cdot;\boldsymbol{\alpha})(\boldsymbol{z};\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon_{2}\rightarrow 0+}\frac{1}{\epsilon_{2}}\{q(\boldsymbol{z}+\epsilon_{2}(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma});\boldsymbol{\alpha})-q(\boldsymbol{z};\boldsymbol{\alpha})\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon_{2}\rightarrow 0+}\frac{1}{\epsilon_{2}}\{\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};\boldsymbol{z}+\epsilon_{2}(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})-\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};\boldsymbol{z}-\boldsymbol{\alpha})\}.

Under the restricted linearity condition
\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};\boldsymbol{h})=\langle g(\boldsymbol{\alpha}),\boldsymbol{h}\rangle,\forall\boldsymbol{h}\in[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\gamma}-\boldsymbol{\alpha}]
, for
\boldsymbol{z}=\boldsymbol{\gamma}+t(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})
with
t\in[0,1)
,

\displaystyle\delta q(\cdot;\boldsymbol{\alpha})(\boldsymbol{z};\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})=

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon_{2}\rightarrow 0+}\frac{1}{\epsilon_{2}}\langle g(\boldsymbol{\alpha}),\boldsymbol{z}+\epsilon_{2}(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})-\boldsymbol{\alpha}-\boldsymbol{z}+\boldsymbol{\alpha}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle g(\boldsymbol{\alpha}),\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}\rangle.

Under the restricted continuity condition
\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha}+\epsilon\boldsymbol{h};\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})=\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}),\forall\boldsymbol{h}\in[\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\gamma}-\boldsymbol{\alpha}]
, for
\boldsymbol{z}=\boldsymbol{\gamma}+t(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})
with
t\in[0,1)
,

\begin{split}&\ \delta q(\cdot;\boldsymbol{\alpha})(\boldsymbol{z};\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})\\
=&\lim_{\epsilon_{2}\rightarrow 0+}\frac{1}{\epsilon_{2}}\bigg\{\lim_{\epsilon_{1}\rightarrow 0+}\frac{1}{\epsilon_{1}}\Big[\psi\big(\boldsymbol{\alpha}+\epsilon_{1}[\boldsymbol{z}+\epsilon_{2}(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})-\boldsymbol{\alpha}]\big)-\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\Big]\\
&~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~-\lim_{\epsilon_{1}\rightarrow 0+}\frac{1}{\epsilon_{1}}\Big[\psi\big(\boldsymbol{\alpha}+\epsilon_{1}(\boldsymbol{z}-\boldsymbol{\alpha})\big)-\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\Big]\bigg\}\\
=&\lim_{\epsilon_{2}\rightarrow 0+}\lim_{\epsilon_{1}\rightarrow 0+}\frac{1}{\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2}}\Big[\psi\big((1-\epsilon_{1})\boldsymbol{\alpha}+\epsilon_{1}\boldsymbol{z}-\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2}\boldsymbol{\gamma}+\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2}\boldsymbol{\beta}\big)-\psi\big((1-\epsilon_{1})\boldsymbol{\alpha}+\epsilon_{1}\boldsymbol{z}\big)\Big]\\
=&\lim_{\epsilon_{2}\rightarrow 0+}\lim_{\epsilon_{1}\rightarrow 0+}\frac{1}{\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\delta\psi\big((1-\epsilon_{1})\boldsymbol{\alpha}+\epsilon_{1}\boldsymbol{z}+\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2}s(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma});\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2}(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})\big)\,\mathrm{d}s\\
=&\lim_{\epsilon_{2}\rightarrow 0+}\lim_{\epsilon_{1}\rightarrow 0+}\int_{0}^{1}\delta\psi\big((1-\epsilon_{1})\boldsymbol{\alpha}+\epsilon_{1}\boldsymbol{z}+\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2}s(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma});\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}\big)\,\mathrm{d}s\\
=&\lim_{\epsilon_{2}\rightarrow 0+}\int_{0}^{1}\lim_{\epsilon_{1}\rightarrow 0+}\delta\psi\big(\boldsymbol{\alpha}+\epsilon_{1}(\boldsymbol{z}+\epsilon_{2}s(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})-\boldsymbol{\alpha});\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}\big)\,\mathrm{d}s\\
=&\ \delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}),\end{split}

where we used the positive homogeneity of
\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};\cdot)
and the dominated convergence theorem. (The integral is well-defined due to the boundedness and Lebesgue measurability of the integrand.)

The two sets of conditions are not equivalent in multiple dimensions. But in either case,
\delta q(\cdot;\boldsymbol{\alpha})(\boldsymbol{z};\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})
is a term independent of
\boldsymbol{z}
.
Hence by Lemma
1
(iv),

\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{q}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\alpha})}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})=\int_{0}^{1}\Big[\delta q(\cdot;\boldsymbol{\alpha})\big(\boldsymbol{\gamma}+t(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma});\,\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}\big)-\delta q(\cdot;\boldsymbol{\alpha})(\boldsymbol{\gamma};\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})\Big]\text{d}t=0.

A.3
Proof of Lemma
3

(i)
Let
\varphi=b^{*}
. Then for all subgradient
\boldsymbol{g}\in\partial\varphi(\boldsymbol{z})
,
(\boldsymbol{g},\boldsymbol{z})
makes a conjugate pair and so
\langle\boldsymbol{g},\boldsymbol{z}\rangle=b(\boldsymbol{g})+\varphi(\boldsymbol{z})
(see, e.g.,
[
45
]
). Using the shorthand notation
(\partial\varphi(\boldsymbol{z}),\boldsymbol{z})
, we represent it as
\langle\partial\varphi(\boldsymbol{z}),\boldsymbol{z}\rangle=b(\partial\varphi(\boldsymbol{z}))+\varphi(\boldsymbol{z})
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}l_{0}(\boldsymbol{\eta};\boldsymbol{z})+b^{*}(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=-\langle\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{\eta}\rangle+b(\boldsymbol{\eta})+\varphi(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=-\langle\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{\eta}\rangle+b(\boldsymbol{\eta})+\langle\boldsymbol{z},\partial\varphi(\boldsymbol{z})\rangle-b(\partial\varphi(\boldsymbol{z}))

\displaystyle=b(\boldsymbol{\eta})-b(\partial\varphi(\boldsymbol{z}))-\langle\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{\eta}-\partial\varphi(\boldsymbol{z})\rangle

\displaystyle=b(\boldsymbol{\eta})-b(\partial\varphi(\boldsymbol{z}))-\langle\nabla b(\partial\varphi(\boldsymbol{z})),\boldsymbol{\eta}-\partial\varphi(\boldsymbol{z})\rangle

\displaystyle={\mathbf{\Delta}}_{b}(\boldsymbol{\eta},\partial\varphi(\boldsymbol{z})).

When
p_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
is minimal,
\mathcal{M}
is full-dimensional and the canonical link
g=(\nabla b)^{-1}
is well-defined on
\mathcal{M}^{\circ}
(Proposition 3.1 and Proposition 3.2 in
[
62
]
can be slightly modified to include the dispersion parameter), and so
(g(\boldsymbol{z}),\nabla b(g(\boldsymbol{z})))
or
(g(\boldsymbol{z}),\boldsymbol{z})
makes a conjugate pair.

(ii) Let
\boldsymbol{\mu}(\boldsymbol{\eta})=\nabla b(\boldsymbol{\eta})
or
\boldsymbol{\mu}
for brevity. It follows that
\boldsymbol{\eta}\in\partial\varphi(\boldsymbol{\mu})
and so

\displaystyle-\langle\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{\eta}\rangle+b(\boldsymbol{\eta})+b^{*}(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=-\langle\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{\eta}\rangle+\langle\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\eta}\rangle-\varphi(\boldsymbol{\mu})+\varphi(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=-\langle\boldsymbol{z}-\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\eta}\rangle-\varphi(\boldsymbol{\mu})+\varphi(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle\geq-\delta\varphi(\boldsymbol{\mu};\boldsymbol{z}-\boldsymbol{\mu})-\varphi(\boldsymbol{\mu})+\varphi(\boldsymbol{z})={\mathbf{\Delta}}_{\varphi}(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{\mu}),

where the inequality is due to
[
45
, Theorem 23.2]
. We claim that the inequality is actually an equality.

Indeed, if there exist
\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\eta}_{2}\in\partial\varphi(\boldsymbol{\mu})
with
\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1}\neq\boldsymbol{\eta}_{2}
, then
\bar{{\mathbf{\Delta}}}_{b}(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{2},\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1})=\langle\nabla b(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{2})-\nabla b(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1}),\boldsymbol{\eta}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1}\rangle=0
and so
{{\mathbf{\Delta}}}_{b}(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{2},\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1})=0
since
b
is convex. Therefore, for any random vector
\boldsymbol{y}
following
p_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
in the exponential family, where
\boldsymbol{\eta}=t\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1}+(1-t)\boldsymbol{\eta}_{2}
,
t\in(0,1)
,

Var((\boldsymbol{\eta}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1})^{T}\boldsymbol{y})=0,

which can be obtained from Proposition 3.1 of
[
62
]
. Because
\exp((\langle\cdot,\boldsymbol{\eta}\rangle-{b}(\boldsymbol{\eta}))/\sigma^{2})>0
for any
\boldsymbol{\eta}\in\Omega
, we have
\langle\boldsymbol{\eta}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}\rangle=c
for almost every
\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}
with respect to
\nu
(i.e.,
p_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
is not minimal). It follows that

\langle\boldsymbol{z}-\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\eta}_{2}\rangle=0.

Finally, from
\delta\varphi(\boldsymbol{\mu};\boldsymbol{h})=\sup\{\langle\boldsymbol{g},\boldsymbol{h}\rangle:\boldsymbol{g}\in\partial\varphi(\boldsymbol{\mu}\}

[
45
, Theorem 23.4]
, the claim is true.

In the case that
p_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
is also minimal,
\varphi
can be shown to be strictly convex and differentiable on
\mathcal{M}^{\circ}

[
45
, Theorem 26.4]
.

(iii) Let
\,\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}=p_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{0}
. By definition,

\mbox{KL}(p_{\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1}},p_{\boldsymbol{\eta}_{2}})=\int\log(\,\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1}}/\,\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\eta}_{2}})\,\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1}}=\int p_{\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1}}\log(p_{\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1}}/\,\mathrm{d}p_{\boldsymbol{\eta}_{2}})\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{0}

and so

\displaystyle\mbox{KL}(p_{\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1}},p_{\boldsymbol{\eta}_{2}})

\displaystyle=\int\log{\{e^{(\langle\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1}\rangle-b(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1}))/\sigma^{2}-c(\boldsymbol{y},\sigma^{2})}}/{e^{(\langle\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{\eta}_{2}\rangle-b(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{2}))/\sigma^{2}-c(\boldsymbol{y},\sigma^{2})}}\}\,\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\int\langle\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\eta}_{2}\rangle-b(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1})+b(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{2})\,\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\{b(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{2})-b(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1})+\int\langle\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\eta}_{2}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}P_{\eta_{1}}\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\{b(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{2})-b(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1})+\langle\nabla b(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1}),\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\eta}_{2}\rangle\}

\displaystyle={\mathbf{\Delta}}_{b}(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{2},\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1})/\sigma^{2},

where the third equality is due to
\,\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{y}\sim p_{\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1}}}\boldsymbol{y}=\nabla b(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1})
under
\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1}\in\Omega^{\circ}
(which can be derived from Proposition 3.1 of
[
62
]
).
Moreover, from Lemma
1
,
\sigma^{2}{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{l_{0}}(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{2},\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1})={\mathbf{\Delta}}_{b}(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{2},\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1})
.

A.4
Proof of Lemma
4

In this proof, all directional derivatives are with respect with
\boldsymbol{\beta}
. The result of (i) is trivial from the construction of
g
. For (ii), by definition, we have
\delta g(\boldsymbol{\beta};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-},\boldsymbol{h})=\delta f(\boldsymbol{\beta};\boldsymbol{h})+\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\beta};\boldsymbol{h})-\delta q(\boldsymbol{\beta};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-},\boldsymbol{h})
with
q(\boldsymbol{\beta};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-})=\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-};\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-})
. It follows from
q(\boldsymbol{\beta};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-})=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}[\psi(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}+\epsilon(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}))-\psi(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-})]/\epsilon
that

\begin{split}\delta q(\boldsymbol{\beta};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-},\boldsymbol{h})=&\lim_{\epsilon^{\prime}\rightarrow 0+}[q(\boldsymbol{\beta}+\epsilon^{\prime}\boldsymbol{h};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-})-q(\boldsymbol{\beta};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-})]/\epsilon^{\prime}\\
=&\lim_{\epsilon^{\prime}\rightarrow 0+}\big\{(1/\epsilon^{\prime})\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}[\psi(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}+\epsilon(\boldsymbol{\beta}+\epsilon^{\prime}\boldsymbol{h}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}))-\psi(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-})]/\epsilon\big\}\\
&-\lim_{\epsilon^{\prime}\rightarrow 0+}\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-};\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-})/\epsilon^{\prime}.\end{split}

When
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}=\boldsymbol{\beta}
,
\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-};\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-})=0
and so

\begin{split}\delta q(\boldsymbol{\beta};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-},\boldsymbol{h})|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}=\boldsymbol{\beta}}&=\lim_{\epsilon^{\prime}\rightarrow 0+}\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}[\psi(\boldsymbol{\beta}+\epsilon(\epsilon^{\prime}\boldsymbol{h}))-\psi(\boldsymbol{\beta})]/(\epsilon\epsilon^{\prime})\\
&=\lim_{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow 0+}[\psi(\boldsymbol{\beta}+\epsilon^{\prime\prime}\boldsymbol{h})-\psi(\boldsymbol{\beta})]/\epsilon^{\prime\prime}\\
&=\,\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\beta};\boldsymbol{h}).\end{split}

The above argument also guarantees the existence of
\delta g(\boldsymbol{\beta};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-},\boldsymbol{h})|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}=\boldsymbol{\beta}}
.
Therefore,
\delta g(\boldsymbol{\beta};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-},\boldsymbol{h})|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-}=\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\delta f(\boldsymbol{\beta};\boldsymbol{h})
for any
\boldsymbol{\beta}
and
\boldsymbol{h}
.

A.5
Proof of Lemma
5

All results in Lemma
1
and Lemma
2
can be formulated for
\mathbf{C}
. For example,

\psi
is convex if and only if
\mathbf{C}_{\psi}\geq 0
,
\mathbf{C}_{a\phi+b\varphi}=a\mathbf{C}_{\phi}+b\mathbf{C}_{\varphi}
,
{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{\psi}\geq\mu{\mathbf{D}}_{2}
implies
\mathbf{C}_{\psi}\geq\mu\mathbf{C}_{2}
since
\mathbf{C}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta},\theta):=\mathbf{C}_{\|\cdot\|_{2}^{2}/2}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta},\theta)=\theta(1-\theta){\mathbf{D}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta})
, and so on. To show (i), we have

\begin{split}&\mathbf{C}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta},\theta)+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\theta\boldsymbol{\alpha}+(1-\theta)\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\alpha})\\
=\,&\theta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha})+(1-\theta)\psi(\boldsymbol{\beta})-\psi(\theta\boldsymbol{\alpha}+(1-\theta)\boldsymbol{\beta})\\
&+\psi(\theta\boldsymbol{\alpha}+(1-\theta)\boldsymbol{\beta})-\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};\theta\boldsymbol{\alpha}+(1-\theta)\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\alpha})\\
=\,&(\theta-1)\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha})+(1-\theta)\psi(\boldsymbol{\beta})-\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};(1-\theta)(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\alpha}))\\
=\,&(1-\theta)\psi(\boldsymbol{\beta})-(1-\theta)\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha})-(1-\theta)\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\alpha})\\
=\,&(1-\theta)\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\alpha}).\end{split}

Similar to the proof of Lemma
2
, let
q(\cdot;\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\delta\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha};\cdot-\boldsymbol{\alpha})
. Then

\mathbf{C}_{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\alpha})}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma},\theta)-\mathbf{C}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma},\theta)=\mathbf{C}_{\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha})+q(\cdot;\boldsymbol{\alpha})}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma},\theta)=\mathbf{C}_{q(\cdot;\boldsymbol{\alpha})}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma},\theta),

without requiring the directional differentiability of
q(\cdot;\boldsymbol{\alpha})
.
We can show analogous results to Lemma
2
.
For example, for any convex
\psi
, from the positively homogenous convexity of
q
,

\mathbf{C}_{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\alpha})}\leq\mathbf{C}_{\psi}

holds for any
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, and for
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(1-\theta)\boldsymbol{\gamma}+\theta\boldsymbol{\beta}
with
\theta\not\in(0,1)
,

\mathbf{C}_{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\alpha})}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})=\mathbf{C}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})

follows from the restricted linearity of
q
. In particular, when
\nabla\psi(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
exists,
q
is linear and so
\mathbf{C}_{q(\cdot;\boldsymbol{\alpha})}\equiv 0
which gives the result in (ii).

A.6
Proof of Theorem
1

The theorem can be proved based on Theorem 6.1 of
[
37
]
and property (iii) of Lemma
3
in Section
2.1
. We give some details for the second conclusion; the proof of the first follows similar lines and is easier. Consider a signal subclass

\mathcal{B}^{1}=\{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}:\beta_{j}\in\{0,\tau R\},\|\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{0}\leq s^{*}\},

where

R=[\sigma(\log(ep/s^{*}))^{1/2}/{\overline{\kappa}}^{1/2}]\wedge M

and
1>\tau>0
is a small constant to be chosen later.
Clearly,
\mathcal{B}^{1}\in\mathcal{B}(s^{*},M)
. By Stirling’s approximation,
\log|\mathcal{B}^{1}|\geq\log{p\choose s^{*}}\geq s^{*}\log(p/s^{*})\geq cs^{*}\log(ep/s^{*})

for some universal constant
c
.

Let
\rho({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{2})=\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{2}\|_{0}
, the Hamming distance between
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1}
and
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{2}
. By Lemma A.3 in
[
44
]
, there exists a subset
\mathcal{B}^{10}\subset\mathcal{B}^{1}
such that
\boldsymbol{0}\in\mathcal{B}^{10}
and

\log|\mathcal{B}^{10}|\geq c_{1}s^{*}\log(ep/s^{*}),\rho({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{2})\geq c_{2}s^{*},\forall{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{2}\in\mathcal{B}^{10},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1}\neq{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{2}

for some universal constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
. Then

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{X}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1}-\boldsymbol{X}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\underline{\kappa}\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{2}\|_{2}^{2}=\underline{\kappa}\tau^{2}R^{2}\rho({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{2})\geq c_{2}\underline{\kappa}\tau^{2}R^{2}s^{*}

(A.6)

for any
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{2}\in\mathcal{B}^{10}
,
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1}\neq{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{2}
.

By Lemma
3
(iii), since
\Omega
is open, for any
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\in\mathcal{B}^{10}
, we have

\mbox{KL}(p_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},p_{{\boldsymbol{0}}})={\mathbf{\Delta}}_{l_{0}}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{X}{\boldsymbol{\beta}})\leq{\tau^{2}}\overline{\kappa}R^{2}s^{*}/(2\sigma^{2}).

Therefore,

\frac{1}{|\mathcal{B}^{10}|-1}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\mathcal{B}^{10}\setminus\{\boldsymbol{0}\}}\mbox{KL}(p_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},p_{{\boldsymbol{0}}})\leq{\tau^{2}}s^{*}\log(ep/s^{*}).

(A.7)

Combining (
A.6
) and (
A.7
) and choosing a sufficiently small value for
\tau
, we can apply Theorem 2.7 of
[
58
]
to get the desired lower bound.

A.7
Proof of Proposition
1

We first introduce a lemma.

Lemma A.2
.

For the sequence of iterates
\{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)}\}

defined by (
31
) starting from an arbitrary point
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(0)}
, if
f(\cdot)
and
g(\cdot;\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})
are directionally differentiable, the following inequality holds for any
\boldsymbol{\beta}
and
t\geq 0

\displaystyle\begin{split}&f(\boldsymbol{\beta})+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})\\
\geq\ &f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)})+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})+(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{\psi}(\cdot;\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})}+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{f})(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)}).\end{split}

(A.8)

It can be proved by Lemma
A.1
and Lemma
1
(details omitted).
Rearranging (
A.8
) gives

\begin{split}&f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)})-f(\boldsymbol{\beta})+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{f}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)})\\
\leq\,&\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})-\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{\psi}(\cdot;\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)}).\end{split}

Under
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{f}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)})\geq 0
, we have

f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)})-f(\boldsymbol{\beta})\leq\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})-\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{\psi}(\cdot;\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)}).

(A.9)

By Lemma
2
, when
\psi
is differentiable,
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{\psi}(\cdot;\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})}
is well-defined and equals
{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{\psi}
. Adding up the corresponding inequality for
t=0,1,\ldots,T
leads to

\sum_{t=0}^{T}[f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)})-f(\boldsymbol{\beta})]\leq\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(0)})-\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{(T+1)}).

Therefore,

\mathop{\mathrm{avg}}_{0\leq t\leq T}f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)})-f(\boldsymbol{\beta})\leq\frac{1}{T+1}[\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(0)})-\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{(T+1)})].

Note that under just the directional differentiability of
{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{\psi}(\cdot;\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})
, (
35
) can be replaced by
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})+(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{f}+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{\psi}(\cdot;\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})}-\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi})(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)})\geq 0,~0\leq t\leq T
.

In the specific case that both
f
and
\psi
are convex, (
35
) is always satisfied by Lemma
1
and letting
\boldsymbol{\beta}=\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)}
in (
A.9
) gives

f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)})-f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})\leq-\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)})\leq 0.

Hence

f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(T+1)})-f(\boldsymbol{\beta})=\min_{0\leq t\leq T}f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)})-f(\boldsymbol{\beta})\leq\mathop{\mathrm{avg}}_{0\leq t\leq T}f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)})-f(\boldsymbol{\beta}).

The proof is complete.

A.8
Proof of Proposition
2

Substituting
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{o}
for
\boldsymbol{\beta}
in Lemma
A.2
gives

\begin{split}&f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)})-f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{o})+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{\psi}(\cdot;\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{o},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)})\\
\leq\,&\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{o},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})-\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{f}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{o},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)}).\end{split}

(A.10)

By Lemma
A.1
, we get

f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)})-f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{o})\geq\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{f}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{o}).

(A.11)

Combining (
A.10
) and (
A.11
) yields

\displaystyle(2\bar{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{f}+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{\psi}(\cdot;\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})})(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{o},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)})+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})\leq\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{o},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)}).

(A.12)

It follows from the strong idempotence property that

(2\bar{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{f}+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi})(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{o},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)})\leq\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{o},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})-\min_{0\leq t\leq T}{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)}),

(A.13)

for any
0\leq t\leq T
,
and so (
40
) can be obtained under
2\bar{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{f}\geq\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}
.

To show the first result, since
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\phi}=\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{f}
, (
A.13
) becomes

\displaystyle(2\bar{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{f}+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\phi}-\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{f})(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{o},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)})\leq(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\phi}-\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{f})(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{o},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})-\min_{0\leq t\leq T}{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)}).

Because
\kappa>1
, (
37
) implies that

\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{f}\geq(\kappa+1)\bar{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{\phi}/\kappa-{\mathrel{\mathop{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\smallsetminus$}\vss}}}_{\phi}}.

Applying the inequality twice, we obtain
((\kappa+1)/\kappa)\bar{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{\phi}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{o},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)})\leq(2-(\kappa+1)/\kappa)\bar{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{\phi}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{o},\allowbreak\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})-\min_{0\leq t\leq T}{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})
,
or

\bar{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{\phi}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{o},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)})\leq\frac{\kappa-1}{\kappa+1}\bar{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{\phi}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{o},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})-\frac{\kappa}{\kappa+1}\min_{0\leq t\leq T}{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)}).

(A.14)

The final conclusion can be obtained by applying (
A.14
) iteratively for
t=0,1,\ldots,T
.

A.9
Proofs of Theorem
2
and Corollary
1

The proof of the theorem follows from Section
A.7
. In fact, setting
\boldsymbol{\beta}=\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)}
in (
A.8
) gives

\displaystyle(\mathrel{\mathop{{\mathbf{\Delta}}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\smallsetminus$}\vss}}}_{\psi}+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{\psi}(\cdot;\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})}+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{f})(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)})\leq f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})-f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)}),

which, by the weak idempotence property (with
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)}
), reduces to

(2\bar{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{\psi}+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{f})(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)})\leq f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})-f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)}).

(A.15)

Summing up (
A.15
) over
t=0,1,\ldots,T
gives the conclusion.

Next, we prove a result slightly more general than Corollary
1
. Recall the surrogate

g(\boldsymbol{\beta};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-})=f(\boldsymbol{\beta})+(\rho{\mathbf{D}}_{\varphi}-{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{f})(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-})

where
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}^{1}
is a strictly convex function, and
f
is continuous and directionally differentiable but not necessarily convex or differentiable. Denote
\arg\min g(\boldsymbol{\beta};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-})
by
\mathcal{T}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-})
.

Corollary
1
’
.

Suppose that
{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{f}\leq L\bar{\mathbf{D}}_{\varphi}
for some
L>0
and the inverse stepsize parameter
\rho
satisfies
\rho>L/2
. Then
\mathop{\mathrm{avg}}_{0\leq t\leq T}(2\rho\bar{\mathbf{D}}_{\varphi}-{\mathrel{\mathop{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\smallsetminus$}\vss}}}_{f}})\allowbreak(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)})\leq\frac{f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(0)})}{(T+1)}
and so
\mathop{\mathrm{avg}}_{0\leq t\leq T}\bar{\mathbf{D}}_{\varphi}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)})\leq\frac{f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(0)})}{(T+1)(2\rho-L)}
.

Moreover, for any accumulation point of
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)}
at which
\mathcal{T}
is continuous, it must be a fixed point of
\mathcal{T}
. This is particularly true when
f\in\mathcal{C}^{1}
.

Observe from (
A.15
) that

\displaystyle f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})-f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)})

\displaystyle\geq(2\rho\bar{{\mathbf{D}}}_{\varphi}-2\bar{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{f}+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{f})(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)})

\displaystyle\geq(2\rho\bar{{\mathbf{D}}}_{\varphi}-\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{f})(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})

\displaystyle\geq(2\rho-L)\bar{{\mathbf{D}}}_{\varphi}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})\geq 0.

The error bounds can be obtained.

Let
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{o}
be the limit point of some subsequence
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{t_{l}}
as
l\rightarrow\infty
. Hence
f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})
converges monotonically to
