\displaystyle{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{f}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}})\leq\langle\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\boldsymbol{\delta}\rangle|_{\boldsymbol{\delta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}}\leq\frac{1}{c}{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{f}(\boldsymbol{\delta}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}})|_{\boldsymbol{\delta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}}+\frac{1}{c}h^{*}(c\boldsymbol{\epsilon})

or
(1-1/c){\mathbf{\Delta}}_{f}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}})\leq h^{*}(c\boldsymbol{\epsilon})/c

for any
c>0
.

On the other hand,

\displaystyle h^{*}(\boldsymbol{\zeta})

\displaystyle=\sup_{\boldsymbol{\delta}}\langle\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{\delta}\rangle-f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}+\boldsymbol{\delta})+f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})+\langle\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}),\boldsymbol{\delta}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sup_{\boldsymbol{\delta}}\langle\boldsymbol{\zeta}+\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}),\boldsymbol{\delta}\rangle-f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}+\boldsymbol{\delta})+f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})

\displaystyle=\sup_{\boldsymbol{\delta}}\langle\boldsymbol{\zeta}+\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}),\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}+\boldsymbol{\delta}\rangle-f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}+\boldsymbol{\delta})+f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})-\langle\boldsymbol{\zeta}+\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}),\boldsymbol{\eta}^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle=f^{*}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}+\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}))+f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})-\langle\boldsymbol{\zeta}+\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}),\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\rangle,

where
f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}),\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})
are known to be finite.
Therefore we obtain

\displaystyle{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{f}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}})\leq\frac{1}{c-1}[f^{*}((c-1)\boldsymbol{\epsilon})+f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})-(c-1)\langle\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\rangle],\ \forall c>0.

(A.61)

Taking
c=2
and using
\,\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}=\boldsymbol{0}
gives the
{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{f}
risk bound (
A.57
).

Next, we prove the second bound under the no-model-ambiguity assumption.
Using the optimality of
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}
, we have further

\displaystyle h^{*}(\boldsymbol{\zeta})

\displaystyle=f^{*}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}+\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}))-f^{*}(-\boldsymbol{\epsilon})-\langle\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\rangle.

Moreover, from the assumption and definition (
A.55
), it is easy to show that
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\in\partial f^{*}(-\boldsymbol{\epsilon})
, and so
\nabla f^{*}(-\boldsymbol{\epsilon})=\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}
, from which it follows that

\displaystyle h^{*}(\boldsymbol{\zeta})={\mathbf{\Delta}}_{f^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}-\boldsymbol{\epsilon},-\boldsymbol{\epsilon}).

(A.62)

Taking
c=2
gives (
A.58
) (even though
\,\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
may not be
\boldsymbol{0}
).

Finally, for any
\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\mathcal{S}
,
f(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}})\leq f({\boldsymbol{\beta}})
and so

\displaystyle{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{f}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})\leq{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{f}({\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})+\langle\boldsymbol{\epsilon},(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}})/\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\|_{2})\rangle\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\|_{2}.

We obtain a general result

\displaystyle({\mathbf{\Delta}}_{f}-\delta{\mathbf{D}}_{2})(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})\leq{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{f}({\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})+\frac{1}{2\delta}[\,\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\Gamma(\boldsymbol{\beta})}\langle\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\boldsymbol{\theta}\rangle]^{2},

(A.63)

for any
\delta>0
.

Based on the regularity condition,

\frac{\delta}{2}{\mathbf{D}}_{2}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})\leq{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{f}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})-\frac{\delta}{2}{\mathbf{D}}_{2}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})\leq{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{f}({\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*})+\frac{1}{\delta}(\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\Gamma(\boldsymbol{\beta})}\langle\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\boldsymbol{\theta}\rangle)^{2}

for any
\delta\leq\mu
. Taking
\delta=\mu
gives the desired result.
∎

B
Algorithms for Accelerations

For clarity, we give an outline of the algorithms for acceleration.

Algorithm B.1

Accelerated Bregman of the second kind

Input

\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(0)}
: initial value;
\rho_{\textrm{min}}>0
,
\alpha>0
,
M\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mu_{0}\geq 0
(e.g.,
\rho_{\textrm{min}}=1
,
\alpha=2
,
M=3
)

1:

\theta_{0}\in(0,1]
,
t\leftarrow 0
,
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(0)}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(0)}
;

2:

while
not converged
do

3:

\rho_{t}\leftarrow\rho_{\min}/\alpha
,
s\leftarrow 0

4:

repeat

5:

s\leftarrow s+1

6:

\rho_{t}\leftarrow\alpha\rho_{t}

7:

if
t\geq 1
, then
\theta_{t}\leftarrow(\sqrt{r^{2}+4r}-r)/2
with
r=(\rho_{t-1}\theta_{t-1}+\mu_{0})\theta_{t-1}/{\rho_{t}}

8:

\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)}\leftarrow(1-\theta_{t})\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)}+\theta_{t}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(t)}

9:

\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(t+1)}\leftarrow\mathop{\arg\min}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\{f(\boldsymbol{\beta})-\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi_{0}}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)}){+}\mu_{0}{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{\phi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)})+\theta_{t}\rho_{t}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\phi}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(t)})\}

10:

\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)}\leftarrow(1-\theta_{t})\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)}+\theta_{t}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(t+1)}

11:

R_{t}\leftarrow\theta_{t}^{2}\rho_{t}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\phi}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(t)})-\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\bar{\psi}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)})

12:

+(1-\theta_{t})\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\bar{\psi}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)})+\mathbf{C}_{f(\cdot)-\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\bar{\psi}_{0}}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)})}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)},\theta_{t})

13:

until

R_{t}\geq 0
or
s>M

14:

if
s>M
, pick
(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)},\rho_{t},\theta_{t})
with the largest
R_{t}/(\theta_{t}^{2}\rho_{t})

15:

t\leftarrow t+1

16:

end

while

17:

return

\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)}
.

Algorithm B.2

Accelerated Bregman of the first kind

Input

\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(0)}
: initial value;
\rho_{\textrm{min}}>0
,
\alpha>0
,
M\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mu_{0}\geq 0
(
\rho_{\textrm{min}}=1
,
\alpha=2
,
M=3
)

1:

\theta_{0}\in(0,1]
,
t\leftarrow 0
;

2:

while
not converged
do

3:

\rho_{t}\leftarrow\rho_{\min}/\alpha
,
s\leftarrow 0

4:

repeat

5:

s\leftarrow s+1

6:

\rho_{t}\leftarrow\alpha\rho_{t}

7:

if
t\geq 1
, then
\theta_{t}\leftarrow(\sqrt{r^{2}+4r}-r)/2
with
r=(\rho_{t-1}\theta_{t-1}+\mu_{0})\theta_{t-1}/{\rho_{t}}

8:

\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)}+\{\rho_{t-1}\theta_{t}(1-\theta_{t-1})/(\rho_{t-1}\theta_{t-1}+\mu_{0})\}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t-1)})

9:

if
t\geq 1
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)}
if
t=0

10:

\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)}\leftarrow\mathop{\arg\min}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\{f(\boldsymbol{\beta})-\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi_{0}}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)})+\mu_{0}\mathbf{D}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)})+\rho_{t}\mathbf{D}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)})\}

11:

R_{t}\leftarrow(\rho_{t}\mathbf{D}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\bar{\psi}_{0}})(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)})+(1-\theta_{t})\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\bar{\psi}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)})

12:

until

R_{t}\geq 0
or
s>M

13:

if
s>M
, pick
(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(t)},\rho_{t},\theta_{t})
with the largest associated
R_{t}/(\theta_{t}^{2}\rho_{t})

14:

t\leftarrow t+1

15:

end

while

16:

return

\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)}
.

C
Experiments

This section performs some simulation studies to support the theoretical results.

C.1
Computational error

In this part, we use mirror descent and DC programming to solve two nonconvex problems.

\bullet
Nonconvex mirror descent for IS divergence minimization

In infrared astronomical satellite (IRAS) image reconstruction
[
13
]
and audio signal processing
[
21
,
35
]
, the Itakura-Saito (IS) divergence (or the negative cross Burg entropy),
\mathrm{IS}(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})=\sum_{i}(a_{i}/b_{i}-\log(a_{i}/b_{i})-1)
, is popularly used to measure the discrepancy between the observed data and the reconstructed data.
Given
\boldsymbol{X}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n\times p}
, and
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}
, the problem can be defined by

\min f(\boldsymbol{\beta}):=\mathrm{IS}(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\beta})~\text{s.t.\,}\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}_{+}
, which is nonconvex in
\boldsymbol{\beta}
.
To maintain the nonnegativity constraint in updating
\boldsymbol{\beta}
automatically, we develop a mirror descent algorithm. Concretely, define

g(\boldsymbol{\beta};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-})=f(\boldsymbol{\beta})+(\rho\mathbf{D}_{\varphi}-\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{f})(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{-})
,
where
\varphi(\boldsymbol{\beta})=\sum_{j}\beta_{j}\log\beta_{j}-\beta_{j}
.
Then, minimizing
g(\boldsymbol{\beta};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})
with respect to
\boldsymbol{\beta}
gives rise to a
multiplicative
rule

\beta_{j}^{(t+1)}=\beta_{j}^{(t)}\exp\Big[-\frac{1}{\rho}\sum_{i}\frac{(\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})_{i}-y_{i}}{(\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})_{i}^{2}}X_{ij}\Big].

(C.1)

From Theorem
2
, the
\mathcal{O}(1/T)
rate of convergence holds for the optimization error
\mathop{\mathrm{avg}}_{0\leq t\leq T}(2\rho\bar{\mathbf{D}}_{\varphi}-{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{f})(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})
. To verify this, we generated a design matrix of size
1000\times 1000
with all elements drawn from
U(0,1)
, and set
\boldsymbol{y}=\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}+\boldsymbol{e}
with
\beta_{j}^{*}
chosen uniformly from the interval
(0,5)
and
e_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
with
\sigma^{2}=10
. We fixed
1/\rho=0.01
.
Figure
C.1
shows how the logarithm of optimization error converges to
-\infty
(since
\log 0=-\infty
) for 50 different
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(0)}
with
\beta^{(0)}_{j}
randomly chosen from
U(0,1)
. Observe that all the error curves in the log-log plot are bounded by a line with slope
-1
.

Fig C.1:
Log-log plot of optimization error v.s. number of iterations: mirror descent for IS divergence minimization with 50 random starting points. All error curves are bounded above by the dashed line which has slope
-1
.

\bullet
DC programming for capped-
\ell_{1}
SVM

High-dimensional classification with concurrent feature selection can be achieved by minimizing a composite objective function. Given
\boldsymbol{X}=[\boldsymbol{x}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{x}_{n}]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}
and
\boldsymbol{y}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}
, let
l(\boldsymbol{\beta})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-y_{i}\boldsymbol{x}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta})_{+}
be the hinge loss
[
61
]
that is nondifferentiable,
and
P(\boldsymbol{\beta};\lambda)=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\min(\lambda|\beta_{j}|,\lambda^{2}/2)
be the capped-
\ell_{1}
penalty
[
66
]
which is nonsmooth and nonconvex.

[
42
]
proposed an effective DC algorithm for solving
\min_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}f(\boldsymbol{\beta}):=l(\boldsymbol{\beta})+P(\boldsymbol{\beta};\lambda)
based on the decomposition
P(\boldsymbol{\beta};\lambda)=d_{1}(\boldsymbol{\beta};\lambda)-d_{2}(\boldsymbol{\beta};\lambda)
with

d_{1}(\boldsymbol{\beta};\lambda)=\lambda\|\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{1},~~d_{2}(\boldsymbol{\beta};\lambda)=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\max(\lambda|\beta_{j}|-\lambda^{2}/2,0).

(C.2)

As stated in Example
4
, we can recharacterize DC as a Bregman-surrogate algorithm

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)}

\displaystyle\in\mathop{\arg\min}f(\boldsymbol{\beta})+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{d_{2}}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})

(C.3a)

\displaystyle\in\mathop{\arg\min}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\max(0,1-y_{i}\boldsymbol{x}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta})+\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{p}\big(|\beta_{j}|-\beta_{j}1_{|\beta_{j}^{(t)}|\geq\lambda/2}\big).

(C.3b)

(
C.3b
) is equivalent to a linear program:

\min_{\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{\beta}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}+\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{p}\zeta_{j}-\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{p}\beta_{j}1_{|\beta_{j}^{(t)}|\geq\lambda/2}

s.t.
\xi_{i}\geq 1-y_{i}\boldsymbol{x}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}
for
i\in[n]
,

-\zeta_{j}\leq\beta_{j}\leq\zeta_{j}
for
j\in[p]
and

\xi_{i},\zeta_{j}\geq 0
for
i\in[n],j\in[p]
, which can be efficiently solved by standard linear programming (LP) solvers. For the convergence of the DC algorithm, a similar result can be shown for the optimization error
\mathrm{avg}_{0\leq t\leq T}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{l}+\mathrel{\mathop{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\smallsetminus$}\vss}}}_{d_{2}}+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{d_{1}})(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)})
, following the lines of the proof of Proposition
4
.

We generated
\boldsymbol{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{400\times 800}
with each row following
\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
and
\Sigma_{ij}=0.5^{|i-j|}
,
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}=[15,10,0,\ldots,0]^{\top}
, and

\boldsymbol{y}=\mathrm{sgn}(\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}+\boldsymbol{e})
where
e_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
with
\sigma^{2}=10
. We fixed
\lambda=1
and ran the DC algorithm for 50 different starting points with each component randomly drawn from
U(0,1)
. The corresponding optimization error curves are plotted in Figure
C.2
, where the
\mathcal{O}(1/T)
rate of convergence is impressive.

Fig C.2:
Log-log plot of optimization error v.s. number of iterations: DC programming for capped-
\ell_{1}
SVM (50 different initial points).

C.2
Statistical error

In this part, we consider two algorithms for sparse regression: LLA and iterative thresholding. The nonconvex “hard” penalty defined by (
55
) is applied, which is constructed from the hard-thresholding rule via (
48
).
Given
\boldsymbol{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}
and
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we study the following regularized problem
\min_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}f(\boldsymbol{\beta}):=l(\boldsymbol{\beta})+\sum_{j=1}^{p}P_{H}(\varrho\beta_{j};\lambda)
,
where
l
is the loss function and
\varrho=\|\boldsymbol{X}\|_{2}
.
The loss functions under consideration are the ordinary quadratic loss and a nonconvex loss which is resistant to gross outliers:

(i)

\ell_{2}
loss:
l(\boldsymbol{\beta})=\|\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{2}^{2}/2
;

(ii)

Tukey’s biweight loss:
l(\boldsymbol{\beta})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{x}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}-y_{i}|}\psi(t)\text{d}t
and
\psi(t)=t[1-(t/c)^{2}]^{2}
if
|t|\leq c
and
0
otherwise,
where
c=4.685\sigma
with
\sigma
a robust estimate of the standard deviation of errors
[
26
]
.

In either case, we have a nonconvex optimization problem. In simulations,
the design matrix
\boldsymbol{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}
has i.i.d. rows drawn from
\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
with
\Sigma_{ij}=0.15^{|i-j|}
, the response is given by
\boldsymbol{y}=\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}+\boldsymbol{e}
with
e_{i}\sim N(0,\sigma^{2})
and
\sigma^{2}=10
, and the regularization parameter
\lambda
is set to
A\sigma\sqrt{\log(ep)}
.
We set
n=800,p=1000,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}=[12,8,0,\ldots,0]^{\top}
and
A=2
.

First, we tested the statistical accuracy of LLA (cf. Theorem
3
and Proposition
5
). We generated 15 initial points
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(0)}
with each element following
U(-a,a)
, with
a\in\{0.5,1,1.5\}
and 5 for each. Figure
C.3
shows how the statistical error varies as the cycles progress, with each curve representing an average over 20 implementations of the same setting. Here, the errors are plotted on a log scale for a better view of the convergence rate. Unlike Figure
C.1
and Figure
C.2
, the statistical errors can not reach 0 (or
-\infty
in the log plot) due to the existence of noise. But they all achieved essentially the same order of statistical precision, which verifies Theorem
3
, and the statistical convergence of LLA was really fast.

Fig C.3:
Log plot of the statistical accuracy of LLA iterates in
P_{H}
-penalized sparse regression (left) and robust regression (right).

On the other hand, the computational burden of LLA turned out to be pretty high, mainly due to the cost of solving a weighted lasso problem at each iteration. We thus turned to iterative thresholding because of its low per-iteration complexity. Figure
C.4
shows some analogous results.
According to Figure
C.4
, all final statistical errors were controlled within the same order of precision. The convergence process seems to conform to the bound in Theorem
5
: when
t
is small,
\log\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})\lesssim-\log(1/\kappa)\,t+\log(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(0)}))
, and when
t
is large,
\log\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)})\lesssim\kappa^{t}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(0)})+\log(\kappa K\lambda^{2}J^{*}/(1-\kappa))
, demonstrating an exponential decay.

Fig C.4:
Log plot of the statistical accuracy of iterative thresholding iterates in
P_{H}
-penalized sparse regression (left) and robust regression (right).

C.3
Accelerations

We test the acceleration schemes in IS divergence minimization and robust sparse regression in this subsection.

Figure
C.5
shows the power of applying the (second) acceleration in IS divergence minimization problem in Section
C.1
, where we used 50 starting points with
\beta_{j}^{(0)}\sim U(0,1),1\leq j\leq p
. With the acceleration, the number of iterations was brought down from 1000 to less than 50 to reach the same value of the objective function, and the overall computational time was saved by nearly 90%.

Fig C.5:
Objective function value (shown on
\log
scale) v.s. number of iterations for the plain and accelerated exponentiated gradient descent algorithms in nonconvex Burg entropy optimization.

Figure
C.6
shows the convergence of statistical error when applying the (first) acceleration scheme in iterative thresholding for the
P_{H}
-penalized Tukey’s loss minimization problem as mentioned in Section
C.2
. The simulation setting remains the same as before and we sampled 20 initial points with
\beta_{j}^{(0)}\sim U(-1,1),1\leq j\leq p
.
A substantial reduction in the number of iterations was achieved. Of course, the line search causes some overhead in computation. But the accelerated iterative thresholding still reduced the overall running time by more than 30%, and obtained slightly better statistical accuracy.

Fig C.6:
Statistical error (shown on
\log
scale) v.s. iteration number for iterative thresholding and accelerated iterative thresholding in robust sparse regression.
